/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/appendix_iterative_comparison.tex:33:  & NLL location drifts monotonically with depth while shape remains invariant, enabling per-generator depth profiling \\
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/limitations.tex:4:\looseness=-1 All eight generators are base (pretrained) models with $\leq$8.2B parameters; instruction-tuned variants show substantially degraded depth signal (within-model 3-class F1 drops 3.7--19.9\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), though QLoRA partially recovers (F1\,=\,0.89--0.91, within-model), suggesting the signal is compressed rather than eliminated.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/table4.tex:2:Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref} and Figure~\ref{fig:ref-comparison} show that performance spread across references is only 0.013 F1, confirming the DDR partition is reference-model agnostic.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/table4.tex:25:\caption{Cross-family reference model comparison. LOGO F1 spread is only 0.013 across three reference models from different families, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/abstract.tex:4:We decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, negative log-likelihood (NLL) location features drift monotonically with depth (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.46$), yielding a ${\geq}8{\times}$ effect-size gap.

1	% Decoding Strategy Robustness
2	To verify that DDR shape stability is not an artifact of nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$, $\tau{=}0.7$),
3	we repeat the generation chain experiment under two alternative decoding strategies:
4	greedy decoding ($\tau{=}0$) and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$, $\tau{=}0.7$).
5	All eight generators spanning five architectural families produce ${\sim}$1{,}000 chains of depth 0--4 per strategy,
6	yielding ${\sim}$5{,}000 texts per generator--strategy combination.
7	
8	Table~\ref{tab:decoding-shape} reports Cohen's $d$ (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) for the three shape features across strategies;
9	Table~\ref{tab:decoding-drift} provides the corresponding values for drifting and intermediate features.

70	\caption{Decoding strategy robustness. \textbf{(a)} Mean Cohen's $d$ (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) across three decoding strategies. Drifting features maintain large $d$ across all strategies; shape features remain below the stationarity threshold ($d < 0.15$). Reference lines mark drifting ($d{=}0.50$) and stationary ($d{=}0.15$) thresholds. \textbf{(b)} Per-generator mean shape-feature $|d|$: all generators stay near-stationary under all three strategies.}
71	\label{fig:decoding-robustness}
72	\end{figure*}
73	
74	% VERSION C — greedy confirms DDR; top-k partially attenuates (ACTIVE)
75	Under greedy decoding ($\tau{=}0$), shape features remain near-stationary across all eight generators
76	(mean $|d|{=}$\textbf{0.05}, max \textbf{0.14}), while core drifting features maintain large effects
77	(mean $d{=}$\textbf{0.94}). This confirms that DDR is an empirical regularity of autoregressive generation
78	independent of sampling truncation.
79	Top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) partially attenuates the partition.
80	Shape features show boundary violations in 4/8 generators under top-$k$; however, these generators exhibit comparable or higher violations under nucleus sampling (Pythia entropy\_max: 0.21--0.25 nucleus vs.\ 0.26--0.27 top-$k$; OLMo tail\_decay: 0.22 under both). These are model-intrinsic boundary effects rather than strategy-induced degradation.
81	Core drifting effect sizes weaken (mean $d{=}$\textbf{0.67} vs.\ \textbf{0.90} under nucleus).
82	This modulation is consistent with the mechanistic account in \S\ref{sec:discussion}:
83	top-$k$ truncation introduces a fixed-width selection window that interacts differently
84	with depth-compressed distributions than nucleus sampling's probability-mass truncation,
85	partially blurring the location/shape boundary.
86	The core DDR partition (drifting features carrying depth signal while shape features
87	provide cross-generator reference) remains intact under both greedy and nucleus sampling
88	and is attenuated but recognizable under top-$k$.
89	Entropy\_std exhibits strong strategy dependence (greedy mean $d{=}$0.83; nucleus 0.46; top-$k$ 0.23). Its depth sensitivity is modulated by the token-selection mechanism: deterministic greedy selection amplifies entropy variance across depths, while top-$k$'s fixed window compresses it. This further supports its classification as an intermediate rather than core feature.
90

1	\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}
2	
3	Table~\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.
4	All results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.
5	
6	\begin{table}[ht]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}
10	\label{tab:instruct}
11	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}
13	\toprule
14	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Binary F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\boldsymbol{\rho}$\textbf{(ent.\ mean)}} & \\
15	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
16	\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \textbf{Rev.} \\
17	\midrule
18	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
19	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
20	Llama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	Instruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\Delta| \leq 3.2$\,pp).
26	The asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.
27	Entropy-mean $\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\ 3 of 7 for Llama.
28	This pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}
31	To test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).
32	
33	\begin{table}[ht]
34	\centering
35	\small
36	\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}
37	\label{tab:instruct-retrain}
38	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
39	\toprule
40	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Base$\rightarrow$Inst} & \textbf{Retrained} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
41	\midrule
42	OLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\
43	Qwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\
44	Qwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	Retraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72). Instruction tuning compresses depth signal beyond what retraining on reversed-polarity features can fully recover.
50	
51	\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}
52	$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\,=\,0.372 (vs.\ 0.660 for base models); the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.
53	The compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).
54	Cross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\,=\,0.490; instruct$\rightarrow$base F1\,=\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.
55	These results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}
60	\caption{DDR partition under base vs.\ instruction-tuned models. (a)~Base models (silhouette\,=\,0.660): clear drifting/non-drifting separation. (b)~Instruction-tuned models (silhouette\,=\,0.372): all features compress toward zero, collapsing the partition; entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate; formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators.}
61	\label{fig:instruct-ddr}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}
65	One hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.
66	To test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.
67	The hypothesis is partially rejected: Qwen3-1.7B-Instruct shows mixed changes (entropy max $d$: $0.51 \to 0.08$, becoming near-stationary; entropy std $d$: $1.07 \to 1.51$, increasing), while OLMo-Instruct loses nearly all depth signal (entropy mean $d$: $1.33 \to 0.02$; tail decay $d$: $0.60 \to 0.06$).
68	The model-dependent heterogeneity rules out reference mismatch as a universal explanation for instruct DDR degradation. Alignment fundamentally reshapes the generation dynamics rather than introducing a correctable measurement artifact.
69	
70	\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}
71	To test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).
72	
73	\begin{table}[ht]
74	\centering
75	\small
76	\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}
77	\label{tab:instruct-qlora}
78	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
79	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
80	\toprule
81	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\
82	\cmidrule(lr){2-3}
83	\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\boldsymbol{\Delta}$ & \textbf{$N$} \\
84	\midrule
85	OLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\,$\pm$\,0.007 & $+$31.3\,pp & 4{,}429 \\
86	Qwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\,$\pm$\,0.008 & $+$17.5\,pp & 4{,}963 \\
87	\midrule
88	OLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\,$\pm$\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\
89	Qwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\,$\pm$\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\
90	\bottomrule
91	\end{tabular}}
92	\end{table}
93	
94	The XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\,pp). Depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.
95	Note that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.
96	All results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.

Let me now systematically go through all the audit dimensions:

### 1. 叙事一致性 (CRITICAL)


The abstract and introduction both identify recursive generation at varying depths as the core problem, with existing detectors only providing binary human-or-machine classification. The method section in both places describes the same DDR decomposition approach focusing on location/shape asymmetry and depth profiling. Now checking the introduction's claimed contributions against what the paper actually delivers - the empirical decomposition across multiple generators appears to align with the stated methodology. families
2. Task formulation and evaluation suite - depth classification, 37K texts, LOGO
3. Scope characterization - temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, etc.

**Method actually does**: 
- Task formulation (§3.1) ✅
- Generation chain construction (§3.2) ✅
- Feature extraction (§3.3) ✅
- Evaluation protocol (§3.4) ✅

**Method's "why" == Intro's "gap"?**: Intro says existing methods only do binary detection, Method formalizes depth classification as ordinal prediction → ✅

**Experiments verify Intro's claims?**:
- Contribution 1 (DDR): §4.2 validates DDR partition ✅
- Contribution 2 (Task + evaluation): §4.1 shows classification results, LOGO ✅
- Contribution 3 (Scope): Temperature sensitivity (§4.2), cross-model performance (§4.1), baselines (§4.3), and appendices covering instruction tuning and domain variations ✅

**Conclusion matches experiments**: The conclusion accurately reflects the key findings—recursive generation produces decomposable depth responses with location features drifting monotonically while shape features stay stable, enabling cross-generator classification (F1=0.694–0.762) and cross-model performance (F1=0.830) at moderate temperatures, plus QLoRA recovery showing promise for instruction-tuned models ✅

**Potential drift**: The Abstract mentions binary F1 scores (0.694 for gen-1 vs. gen-2+, 0.830 cross-model) that need alignment with the ordinal classification framing presented in the method.

I'm noticing a discrepancy in the cost-efficiency claim—the Introduction states the 7-feature ensemble matches multi-detector baselines at 2.4× lower cost, but comparing Table 2 (LOGO, F1=0.694) against Table 3 (baselines, F1=0.705–0.708) shows these come from different evaluation protocols, so the comparison needs clarification on which baseline it's actually matching. I'm noticing that Table 2 and Table 3 both seem to report 8-generator LOGO results, but with different numbers, which raises a question about what's actually being compared in each table. I see the issue now—Table 2 uses macro-averaging across generators while Table 3 pools all predictions together before computing F1, which explains why 0.694 and 0.705 differ for what appears to be the same method. This inconsistency in aggregation methods could be misleading since the paper presents both values without clearly distinguishing between the two approaches. The issue is that the Introduction conflates two different metrics: it cites the 0.694 macro-average F1 from Table 2 but then claims this "matches" the multi-detector ensembles, when the actual comparison should be between the pooled F1 scores of 0.705 versus 0.708 from Table 3. The results section correctly frames this matching claim using the pooled numbers, but the Introduction mixes them together, creating a narrative inconsistency.

Looking at the Abstract, it covers all four key elements: the problem (recursive generation reshapes distributions), the method (DDR decomposition), specific results (F1 numbers), and significance (instruction tuning compresses DDR). The Introduction follows a rough five-paragraph structure that establishes background, identifies the gap in existing work, presents the method and results, and lists contributions—though it's somewhat compressed since the problem importance gets folded into the opening paragraph rather than standing alone.

The Related Work section is well-structured, organizing by topic (AI detection, model collapse, diversity/stylometry/provenance) rather than listing individual papers, which clearly positions this work relative to others.

For contributions and scope alignment, I'm checking that the three main contributions map to specific sections: the empirical DDR decomposition appears in §4.2 with supporting figures, the task formulation and evaluation suite is in §3.1-3.4 with Table 2, and scope characterization is covered in §4.2 plus appendices. The Discussion and Conclusion don't introduce new contributions—they interpret results and summarize—so there are no orphan sections. The title could be more explicit about why this matters, though it does capture the core concept of depth profiling for recursive generation.

Now I'm verifying that claims match their evidence. The "significantly underperform" claim has McNemar p < 0.001 support, there are no unsupported state-of-the-art claims, and the ~2.4× lower cost claim checks out: 22.8 ms/text divided by 9.7 ms/text gives 2.35×. But I'm noticing a potential issue with one of the main claims that needs closer examination.

The paper says the 7-feature approach "matches" the 4-detector ensemble, but the F1 scores show a 0.3 percentage point gap (0.705 vs 0.708). While the confidence intervals do overlap in Figure 3, which technically supports equivalence, the paper doesn't provide a p-value for this direct comparison, so calling it a "match" is borderline—it relies on the visual overlap of CIs rather than explicit statistical testing. For the experimental rigor, the main results table includes 95% CIs and per-generator breakdowns, which is solid, but the baseline table lacks individual method CIs despite the caption mentioning bootstrap confidence intervals for the main method. The baselines themselves are recent and appropriate: FastDetectGPT and Binoculars from 2024, plus LD-Score and Avg-Log.

The ablation studies are thorough—they include feature-level ablation with LOFO analysis and group-level ablation in the appendix, and the number of components tested (7 features, 3 groups) aligns with the method description. For reproducibility, the paper discloses XGBoost hyperparameters (n_estimators=200, max_depth=6, learning rate=0.1), GPU specifications (4× NVIDIA RTX 5090, 62 GPU-hours), data preprocessing details (37,058 texts after filtering), and software versions in the ethics statement.

For the NLP checklist, the paper includes the required Limitations, Ethics, and AI Assistants sections, though LP 2026 expects the full Responsible NLP Checklist to be submitted separately in OpenReview. Now I'm checking terminology consistency across the paper—entropy is properly defined as per-token NLL rather than information-theoretic entropy, and acronyms like DDR, LOGO, and LOFO are used consistently. 1	Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?
2	We fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\ref{tab:downstream}).
3	
4	\begin{table}[ht]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}
8	\label{tab:downstream}
9	\begin{tabular}{lrc}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{Test PPL} \\
12	\midrule
13	All depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
14	Size-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
15	Depth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
16	Binary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
17	Depth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	The results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.
23	Training on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).
24	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).
25	Cross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, which confirms the robustness of this null result.
26	
27	Restricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\,=\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.
28	
29	\paragraph{Equivalence testing.}
30	We apply a two one-sided $t$-test (TOST) with a $\pm$2\% relative margin (equivalence bounds $\pm$0.047 on the ppl scale) to formally assess whether the depth-aware and random subsampling conditions produce practically indistinguishable perplexity.
31	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951): size-matched (random) ppl\,=\,2.367 vs.\ depth 0--1 ppl\,=\,2.387, $\Delta$\,=\,0.020.
32	TOST confirms equivalence ($p = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}$); the two curation strategies yield practically indistinguishable downstream quality within the $\pm$2\% margin.
33	Cross-seed standard deviation is below 0.001 for both variants.
34	
35	Comparing the full dataset ($n$\,=\,12{,}299, ppl\,=\,2.335) against the depth-filtered subset ($n$\,=\,3{,}951, ppl\,=\,2.387): TOST \emph{rejects} equivalence (minimum equivalence margin\,=\,0.053, exceeding the $\pm$2\% bound), which confirms that data quantity drives perplexity reduction beyond what depth-aware selection can recover.
36	
37	Knowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training; at fixed sample count, depth-aware curation is statistically equivalent to uninformed random subsampling.
38	
39	\subsection*{Margin Sensitivity Analysis}
40	
41	To assess the robustness of the equivalence conclusion to margin choice, we repeat the TOST procedure across multiple equivalence margins \cite{lakens_equivalence}, following the recommendation to report sensitivity across plausible bounds rather than relying on a single threshold (Table~\ref{tab:tost-margins}).
42	
43	\begin{table}[ht]
44	\centering
45	\small
46	\caption{TOST equivalence testing across multiple margins. ``Equiv?'' indicates whether equivalence is established at $\alpha = 0.05$.}
47	\label{tab:tost-margins}
48	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%

50	\toprule
51	\textbf{Comparison} & \textbf{Margin} & \textbf{Equiv?} & \textbf{TOST $p$} \\
52	\midrule
53	Full (12{,}299) vs.\ Random (3{,}951) & $\pm$1\% & No & 1.0 \\
54	                                        & $\pm$2\% & Yes & $1 \times 10^{-6}$ \\
55	                                        & $\pm$3\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
56	                                        & $\pm$5\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
57	\midrule

59	                                            & $\pm$2\% & No & 1.0 \\
60	                                            & $\pm$3\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
61	                                            & $\pm$5\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\

63	\end{tabular}}

66	The full dataset vs.\ random subsample comparison passes equivalence at the $\pm$2\% margin ($p = 10^{-6}$), while the full vs.\ depth 0--1 comparison requires a $\pm$3\% margin.

68

70	These appear large but are unreliable: with only $n$\,=\,3 seeds per condition, the pooled standard deviation is extremely small ($<$0.001), artificially inflating $d$.
71	The inflated effect sizes reflect low seed-level variance (confirming reproducibility) rather than practically meaningful perplexity differences.
72	The TOST framework with pre-specified relative margins provides a more appropriate assessment than Cohen's $d$ in this low-seed regime.

\\\\\nSize-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\nBinary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.\nTraining on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \\emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).\nCross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, which confirms the robustness of this null result.\n\nRestricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\\,=\\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence testing.}\nWe apply a two one-sided $t$-test (TOST) with a $\\pm$2\\% relative margin (equivalence bounds $\\pm$0.047 on the ppl scale) to formally assess whether the depth-aware and random subsampling conditions produce practically indistinguishable perplexity.\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951): size-matched (random) ppl\\,=\\,2.367 vs.\\ depth 0--1 ppl\\,=\\,2.387, $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.020.\nTOST confirms equivalence ($p = 1.1 \\times 10^{-4}$); the two curation strategies yield practically indistinguishable downstream quality within the $\\pm$2\\% margin.\nCross-seed standard deviation is below 0.001 for both variants.\n\nComparing the full dataset ($n$\\,=\\,12{,}299, ppl\\,=\\,2.335) against the depth-filtered subset ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951, ppl\\,=\\,2.387): TOST \\emph{rejects} equivalence (minimum equivalence margin\\,=\\,0.053, exceeding the $\\pm$2\\% bound), which confirms that data quantity drives perplexity reduction beyond what depth-aware selection can recover.\n\nKnowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training; at fixed sample count, depth-aware curation is statistically equivalent to uninformed random subsampling.\n\n\\subsection*{Margin Sensitivity Analysis}\n\nTo assess the robustness of the equivalence conclusion to margin choice, we repeat the TOST procedure across multiple equivalence margins \\cite{lakens_equivalence}, following the recommendation to report sensitivity across plausible bounds rather than relying on a single threshold (Table~\\ref{tab:tost-margins}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{TOST equivalence testing across multiple margins. ``Equiv?'' indicates whether equivalence is established at $\\alpha = 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:tost-margins}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Comparison} & \\textbf{Margin} & \\textbf{Equiv?} & \\textbf{TOST $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (12{,}299) vs.\\ Random (3{,}951) & $\\pm$1\\% & No & 1.0 \\\\\n                                        & $\\pm$2\\% & Yes & $1 \\times 10^{-6}$ \\\\\n                                        & $\\pm$3\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n                                        & $\\pm$5\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (12{,}299) vs.\\ Depth 0--1 (3{,}951) & $\\pm$1\\% & No & 1.0 \\\\\n                                            & $\\pm$2\\% & No & 1.0 \\\\\n                                            & $\\pm$3\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n                                            & $\\pm$5\\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe full dataset vs.\\ random subsample comparison passes equivalence at the $\\pm$2\\% margin ($p = 10^{-6}$), while the full vs.\\ depth 0--1 comparison requires a $\\pm$3\\% margin.\nIn absolute terms, perplexity differences are small: $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.032 (full vs.\\ random, 1.4\\% of baseline) and $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.052 (full vs.\\ depth 0--1, 2.2\\% of baseline).\n\nCohen's $d$ values are 86.0 (full vs.\\ random) and 119.7 (full vs.\\ depth 0--1).\nThese appear large but are unreliable: with only $n$\\,=\\,3 seeds per condition, the pooled standard deviation is extremely small ($<$0.001), artificially inflating $d$.\nThe inflated effect sizes reflect low seed-level variance (confirming reproducibility) rather than practically meaningful perplexity differences.\nThe TOST framework with pre-specified relative margins provides a more appropriate assessment than Cohen's $d$ in this low-seed regime.\nHowever, with only $n{=}3$ seeds per condition, the TOST results have limited generalizability: the test is valid given observed variance but the conclusion generalizes over only three random seeds. We characterize this as a preliminary observation rather than a definitive null result.\n",

      "totalLines": 74
    }

1	\subsection{9-Generator Scale Evaluation}\label{app:9gen-scale}
2	
3	This analysis extends the 8-generator LOGO evaluation to nine generators by adding Gemma-2-2B, a model from a previously unrepresented architecture family (Gemma).
4	All results use class-balanced LOGO; the 8-gen averages here (0.770/0.701) differ slightly from Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} (0.762/0.694), which uses an unfiltered protocol.
5	Table~\ref{tab:9gen-logo} reports per-generator LOGO performance under this expanded set.
6	
7	\begin{table}[ht]
8	\centering
9	\caption{9-generator LOGO evaluation. Each row holds out the listed generator at test time. 3-class: gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+; binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+. 95\% bootstrap CIs in brackets.}
10	\label{tab:9gen-logo}
11	\small
12	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
13	\begin{tabular}{llr cc cc}
14	\toprule
15	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
16	\midrule
17	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.857 & [0.848, 0.866] & 0.785 & --- \\
18	Qwen3-0.6B      & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.819 & [0.807, 0.830] & 0.743 & --- \\
19	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & 0.812 & [0.801, 0.822] & 0.728 & --- \\
20	Qwen3-1.7B      & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.811 & [0.800, 0.823] & 0.746 & --- \\
21	OLMo-2-1B       & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.773 & [0.762, 0.784] & 0.692 & --- \\
22	Llama-3.2-3B    & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.759 & [0.748, 0.770] & 0.680 & --- \\
23	Qwen3-8B        & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.696 & [0.683, 0.709] & 0.647 & [0.631, 0.664] \\
24	Gemma-2-2B      & Gemma   & 2.6B & 0.628 & [0.622, 0.634] & 0.440 & [0.432, 0.449] \\
25	Mistral-7B      & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.605 & [0.593, 0.617] & 0.534 & --- \\
26	\midrule
27	\textbf{Average (9-gen)} & & & \textbf{0.751} & & \textbf{0.666} & \\
28	\textbf{Average (8-gen)} & & & \textbf{0.770} & & \textbf{0.701} & \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}}
31	\end{table}
32	
33	\begin{figure}[t]
34	\centering
35	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_9gen_scale.pdf}
36	\caption{Per-generator 3-class LOGO F1 for the 9-generator evaluation. Gemma-2-2B (orange) is the weakest held-out generator. Dashed line: 9-gen average (0.751). Parameter counts shown in parentheses.}
37	\label{fig:9gen-scale}
38	\end{figure}
39	
40	\paragraph{Overall comparison.}
41	Adding Gemma-2-2B reduces the mean 3-class LOGO F1 from 0.770 (8-gen) to 0.751 (9-gen), a drop of $\Delta = -0.019$.
42	The per-generator F1 values for the original eight generators shift by at most $[-0.011, +0.002]$; the inclusion of Gemma data in training does not destabilize existing generalization patterns.
43	
44	\paragraph{Gemma-2-2B anomaly.}
45	Gemma-2-2B is the weakest held-out generator: 3-class F1\,=\,0.628, binary F1\,=\,0.440.
46	Per-class breakdown reveals a striking asymmetry: gen-0 F1\,=\,0.991 and gen-2+ F1\,=\,0.856, but gen-1 F1\,=\,0.036; the classifier almost entirely fails to identify first-generation Gemma continuations.
47	This is not a data-quality artifact: the Gemma-2-2B partition contains 4{,}957 instances with a balanced depth distribution ($\sim$990 per level).
48	A plausible explanation is that Gemma-2's first-generation continuations occupy a region of feature space highly similar to human-written text (gen-0), causing systematic misclassification of gen-1 as gen-0 or gen-2+.
49	This constitutes an architecture-specific generalization limitation rather than a systemic failure of the depth profiling approach.
50	Several architectural differences may explain this behavior: (a)~Gemma-2's distinct attention mechanism and SentencePiece tokenizer with a larger vocabulary may produce first-generation continuations whose feature-space signature closely resembles human text, causing systematic gen-0/gen-1 confusion; (b)~this anomaly does not affect DDR's validity for the remaining eight generators, where per-generator F1 shifts by at most 0.011 upon Gemma inclusion.
51	
52	\paragraph{Scale effect.}
53	A clear parameter-count gradient emerges across the nine generators: three of four sub-1.7B generators achieve F1\,$>$\,0.81 (OLMo-2-1B at 0.773 is the exception), mid-range models (2--3B) cluster at 0.63--0.81, and large models (7B+) fall to 0.60--0.70.
54	Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters) achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.696 under the 9-gen split, compared to 0.701 in the class-balanced 8-gen evaluation; this small difference reflects the changed training composition rather than a property of the Qwen3-8B generator itself.
55	
56	\paragraph{14B-parameter evidence.}
57	Preliminary evidence from a 14.7B generator (Qwen3-14B-Base) confirms that the DDR partition persists at larger scale: Cohen's $d$ for drifting features remains in the large-effect range ($d{=}0.78$--$1.12$), though Spearman $|\rho|$ attenuates by 21--35\%, with entropy mean falling just below the drifting threshold ($|\rho|{=}0.39 < 0.40$, $d{=}0.78$).
58	This scale-dependent signal compression is consistent with larger models producing more self-typical outputs that narrow inter-depth contrasts; the partition structure is preserved even as absolute effect sizes compress.
59	
```
